These conclusions had been poured and left to set. To Douglas, the meeting was a formality. He'd already decided what had happened to his daughter. He needed a professional to confirm it and act.

His hands moved before his mouth did. One palm pressed flat against the coat on his knee — brief, a reflex, the small self-steadying a man does before he says the thing he's been carrying. When he looked up, the destination was already in him. Wherever he thought she was, he'd been there for eleven days.

He said, "Celeste is nineteen. About eight months ago she had cosmetic genework done — cat ears, functional ones, not cosmetic-only. Against what her mother and I wanted. We argued, and then we stopped, because it was already done." A pause, but not an uncertain one. "Six days ago she told us she was going away for a few days. A retreat, she called it. She wouldn't say where. Four days ago she went offline completely. She isn't answering."

Rennick picked up his pen. He set it down without using it. Then: "Neuretics ping?"

"Not responding."

"Location services?"

"Off. Or blocked."

"What's your read on why?"

Douglas's expression moved in one clear direction. He said, "That's why I'm here."

Rennick weighed it.

Rennick opened the briefing.

Douglas had done his research. The pages were laid out the way a frightened, intelligent man lays out information: news items, community alerts, a Pulse Network investigative segment — eighteen months of documented cases where unlicensed modification corridors had fronted for organ operations. The articles were real. The facts inside them were real. But they'd been gathered one careful piece at a time to prove a conclusion that came first. Douglas had decided what he was afraid of, then gone looking for the evidence.

Rennick was on page three when Douglas leaned in and turned ahead to page five. Not aggressive — the move of a man who'd been waiting to reach the part that mattered. "This one," he said. "The Cermak corridor." His finger rested on the header.

Rennick kept his hand on page three. He let the pause sit. Then he set the briefing on the desk and looked at Douglas until Douglas took his hand back.

Across the room, Tamsin had set down her pen and gone still at the intake desk, reading the two clients the same way Rennick was reading the briefing.

Douglas said, "There's a corridor in Pilsen. Unlicensed shops, no provenance checks, clients who disappear into the system and don't come out. She went looking for a modification and found something that found her first. That's the theory."

He said it carefully, using the word theory the way people use it when they mean fact.

Rennick set the briefing down. He said, "Mr. Hartley, before your daughter went dark — was there anyone she'd been in regular contact with? Someone outside your immediate circle?"

Douglas said, "She had friends. Her generation has the feeds."

"Anyone specific? Someone she'd been talking to for several months, maybe longer?"

From the armchair, Analog's pen stopped. Mid-stroke, mid-line. He didn't start again.

A silence. Not long. Douglas said, "There was a boy. Jace. He passed away — eight months ago, nine. A Network accident."

Rennick picked up his pen.

Douglas said, "The corridor is where this started. Whatever the boy was to her before, he's nine months gone. He's not relevant."

Rennick set the pen back down. He hadn't written anything. He'd heard unlicensed corridor before, and it meant one thing: Splicers working below the licensing floor, the kind of shop with two chairs, an autoclave, and no record of anything that went wrong.

He said, "The corridor in Pilsen. Tell me which corridor."

From the armchair, Analog's pen still hadn't moved.

Mei-Lin moved. Not fast, and not like someone who'd been waiting for permission. Like someone who'd been waiting for the room to clear so she could get to work. She set her cup on Tamsin's coaster, took out her own device, and held it flat so Rennick could read.

"I found this through a friend of a friend of hers," she said. "It's semi-private. Eight months of posts."

Rennick reached for the device. Mei-Lin didn't let go.

"You read it here," she said. "I'm not sending it anywhere, and you're not copying it. That's the deal, or it's not a deal."

"I can't work off something I can't take with me."

"Then you can't work off it." Her thumb stayed on the corner of the screen, ready to blank it. "You've spent this whole conversation deciding whether I'm a problem to manage. I've been deciding whether you're someone I hand my sister's private thinking to, or someone I show it to once so you understand what you're actually looking at. Those aren't the same thing. Pick which one you get."

He held her eyes a moment longer than was comfortable for either of them, then took his hand back.

Rennick read.

This wasn't a kid stumbling onto something new. No breathless excitement. No claiming she'd found a secret the world had kept from her. It was a research log. Careful, specific, built one entry at a time — the writing of someone who'd spent months learning to talk about a thing accurately. Baseline drift. Willing flesh. Integration stays. The unlicensed corridor. Ghost period. Motor map formation.

Eight months of it. The thinking had started long before the writing did.

Rennick handed the device back. "She knew what she was doing."

"Yes," Mei-Lin said.

A beat. Then: "Find her."

She said it the way a professional gives an order — flat, complete, no conditions hanging off the end. Rennick heard it that way. He also heard what sat under it. This was a mother who had read eight months of her daughter's research, understood every word, and was still afraid. Her fear had a different shape than her husband's. Douglas was afraid of the wrong story. Mei-Lin was afraid of the right one.

"There's a problem with that," Rennick said, and watched something close behind her eyes. "The research she left behind — the eight months you've read — it's the reason I can't just walk out that door and go looking. Whatever she found, someone else wanted it enough to make her disappear. If I start pulling on those threads, I pull the same people down on us that pulled on her. On you. On Douglas." He set his hands flat on the table. "The fastest way to find your daughter runs straight through the thing that took her. I can go slow and quiet and maybe never get there. Or I can go loud and probably get there and probably bring the same weather down on this house. You've read the work. You already know which way it points. So tell me — knowing that — do you still want me to find her, or do you want me to find her *safely*, because those aren't the same order, and I need you to pick one."

Mei-Lin did not answer right away. Then she said, "Find her." Flat. Complete. No conditions.

"We will," Rennick said. He meant it as a promise and as a fact. He said it the same way for both.

Douglas mentioned it almost in passing — the way you mention a thing you assume everyone already knows. He'd also hired Meridian Consulting. A corporate investigative firm. Full team, comprehensive coverage, already two days into the case. He said it the way people talk about insurance: redundancy, the responsible move when the stakes are high enough.

Rennick kept his face where it was.

From the armchair, not looking up from his notebook, Analog said: "Two days ahead, working which angle?"

Douglas said: "The Pilsen corridor. The organ operation theory. They have contacts in Arcturus organized crime."

Arcturus was the CorpoNation that ran security that far south — the closest thing to law down there. So "Arcturus organized crime" really meant the crime a CorpoNation allowed, taxed, or ran, depending on the week. Douglas offered it like it proved Meridian had reach. Rennick read it the other way.

Analog made a small sound that wasn't yes and wasn't no. He wrote something down. It wasn't for Douglas.

Rennick decided to test the boundary of the arrangement while it was still soft — better to know now whether he was running the case or auditing someone else's. "If they're two days in, we'll want their files. Contact list, interview notes, whatever they've logged out of Pilsen. Otherwise we're both walking the same ground and billing you twice for it."

Douglas didn't reach for a comm. He set his hands flat on the desk, the small settling of a man who'd already rehearsed this part. "Meridian's engagement is separate. Their reporting comes to me, not laterally. I'm not going to be the one who tells a firm like that how to hold their confidences." He smiled the way you smile at a smaller inconvenience. "You'll run your own line. I'd prefer you didn't compare notes, actually. I get cleaner readings when the two of you don't know what the other's found."

So that was the shape of it. Not a partner and not a backstop — a second horse in a race he wasn't going to be told the rules of, run by a client who'd just admitted, out loud and pleasantly, that he wanted them blind to each other. Whatever Meridian turned up first, Rennick would learn about it the way he'd learned about Meridian: in passing, too late to change what it meant.

Analog wrote something else down. This time he underlined it.

Douglas said: "I assume that's not a problem. Parallel investigation. The more coverage the better."

Rennick said: "No problem."

He was watching Douglas when he said it. He was also aware of Mei-Lin — aware that she'd gone very still, the stillness of someone who'd just had a guess confirmed. Her husband had hired Meridian two days ago to work the organ angle, then come here this morning. The order of it told the story. Douglas had already reached his conclusion and paid Meridian to back it up. Then he'd come to Rennick Investigations for the other answer — hedging his bet without admitting that's what it was.

Tamsin stood up from the intake desk. "When she first told you she wanted the ears — what did she say?"

Douglas said: "That it was something she'd wanted for a long time. We thought she'd let it go. She didn't."

"Did she say why functional? Why not cosmetic-only?"

A pause. Rennick watched Douglas find the answer, and then find something uncomfortable sitting right next to it.

"She said she wanted to hear the way Jace heard," Douglas said. "He'd had them since he was sixteen. She said the world sounded different to him." He stopped. "I told her it was grief talking. That in a few months she'd feel differently. That was almost a year ago. She never did."

The room took that in without a word.

Rennick got it before he could put words to it. Not the genework. Not the money. The want under both — a girl rebuilding the inside of her own skull so she could hear the world the way someone she'd lost had heard it. He knew that shape. He knew what it cost, and why a person paid it anyway. And he knew the other thing, the one Douglas had decided not to say: a grieving person who needed no one's permission would go right on needing no one's permission.

He'd worked a case like it once, early in his Arcturus years. That same want had been the last clear thing someone wrote down before the end. They'd closed the file as voluntary. He'd let them. He'd thought about it maybe twice a year since.

For one breath he wasn't a man being paid to find a girl. Then the breath passed, and it was a case again.

Tamsin had watched him do it. She always did. She never said so.

Rennick stood and poured himself water from the carafe on the side table — not because he was thirsty, but because standing gave him a reason to look at both clients at once without making it obvious.

Douglas sat forward, hands on his knees, ready to receive a briefing. Mei-Lin had her coffee. She hadn't moved from the window; she'd turned down the chair. She was watching her husband in a way he hadn't noticed, and the ease of it told Rennick she'd been doing it a long time.

He set the water down. "We're going to ask you a few more questions, and then we'll take the case. Before we do — can you send me Celeste's neuretics ID, her full contact records for the six weeks before she went dark, and anything you have on Jace Dalton? Full name, last known address, any surviving contacts."

Douglas said: "I can send the neuretics and her contact records. I don't keep information on her friends."

Mei-Lin said: "I have Jace's last address and his cohort from the university. I'll send them."

Douglas looked at her. It was the look of a man learning something in public.

Rennick said: "Mrs. Hartley — I'll want to speak with you separately. Before the end of the week."

Douglas said: "Is that—"

"Standard practice when clients have different information," Rennick said, easy, the way you state a procedure. "I'll have Tamsin set a time."

He wouldn't normally open a private channel during intake. He'd wait for the case to develop, for the split to sharpen. He opened it now, in front of Douglas, because Mei-Lin had known about Jace and had been waiting nine months for someone to ask.

He sent them out with the receipt — Φ2,500 retainer, standard terms, the invoice formal and correct on paper. Douglas took it the way a man takes an invoice when invoices are how he closes a deal. Mei-Lin folded her copy once and put it in her bag.

At the door Douglas paused, one hand on the frame. "One thing," he said, not turning all the way around. "Whatever you turn up — it comes to me first. Not her. That's in the terms." Behind him Mei-Lin's hand stilled on the strap of her bag, her copy of the same invoice half into it, and she said nothing at all — which was its own kind of answer. Rennick let the silence run. The contract on his desk didn't say that anywhere. Neither of them corrected the other, and neither of them looked at him for a ruling.

They left the way they'd come. The buzzer sounded once at street level, then the door.

Rennick listened to the footsteps drop away, three flights down. He gave it thirty seconds after the street door closed. Then he said: "Two jobs in one contract. Who's reading what."

Analog said: "He hired Meridian to confirm the organ angle. He hired us because some part of him doesn't believe the organ angle." He turned a page in his notebook without writing on it. "Which isn't the same as him being right."

"He's not right," Corvin said. He said it flat, as a data point. "The research log the mother showed you. Did it reference any of the front operations in the briefing?"

Rennick said: "No. The language was different. Practitioner vocabulary, not somebody scared by the news."

"Someone who'd done that much research," Corvin said, "would have stayed clear of exactly the shops in the news. She went to a reputable operator for the ears eight months ago and came through clean. The briefing is a map of where she didn't go. It tells us nothing about where she went four days ago."

Analog said: "The ears she walked away from clean. Wherever she went four days ago, we don't have an address, a reason, or a name. That's the part I don't like."

"Then we start with the operator who did the ears," Rennick said. "Reputable means records. Records means a next step — a referral, a name she trusted." He pulled up the clinic's listing and keyed the contact line while the other two watched.

The line connected on the second ring to a recorded voice, flat and administrative. The practice had been dissolved six weeks ago pending a licensing review; all patient records were sealed with the medical board; no forwarding contact would be provided. The message repeated, then cut to a dead tone.

Rennick set the phone down. "The one clean thread she left us," he said, "somebody already pulled."

"That's why we're taking it," Rennick said.

Tamsin had pulled the intake form up on her screen and was already working backward through it — not reviewing it, reorganizing it, the way she did when a case had more shape than the client had given it. Without looking up she said: "Analog. The research log. Practitioner vocabulary — what kind of practitioner?"

Analog set his pen down. "Genomics-adjacent. The terms she used for the cochlear architecture — that wasn't consumer copy. She was reading sourced literature. Grad-level, maybe clinical."

"So she had access," Tamsin said.

"Or someone gave her access," Analog said. "Which is different."

The distinction landed and stayed. Rennick turned it over. A grieving girl who'd spent eight months reading clinical work on cochlear genomics. Who'd cleared one procedure clean. Who'd then gone somewhere four days ago that nobody could name. The question wasn't whether she'd been careful. She had been careful. The question was what careful people do when careful stops being enough — when the want under it has been patient for eight months and finally runs out of patience. He knew the answer. He'd known it since the intake. He wasn't going to let this file close the way he'd let one close, once, early in his Arcturus years.

"I'll pull the licensed genomics operators in the Gray Zone," Tamsin said. "Anything with the sourcing to match that literature level."

"Start with the ones that don't advertise," Rennick said.

Tamsin had been quiet — the way she got when a room handed her more than had landed yet. Now she said: "The boy. Jace. Network diver, died eight months ago. She got his ears so she could hear the way he heard."

Rennick said: "The father called it grief talking."

"It isn't," Tamsin said. "Grief talking is wanting him back. This is wanting to stand where he stood. That's grief working." She squared the receipts under her hand without looking at them. "She had a plan. You don't get a functional bilateral hearing mod on a whim. Months. Money. A practitioner. She built toward it."

She said it without dressing it up. She called it a plan because that's what it was.

"So we find the practitioner," she said. "Whoever did the mod knew what she was chasing. I want a name and I want it before she goes wherever the ears are telling her to go."

Tamsin said: "I want the room."

Everyone looked at her.

"Celeste's room," she said. "Before anyone else goes in. If she planned this over months, if she was building toward it — rooms hold that. Resolve looks different from panic. I can tell you which one was in that room."

Analog said: "You think the father would let you?"

Tamsin said: "I think the mother would."

Rennick said: "We'll ask the mother." He wrote it down. Actual paper — Analog kept a spare pad on the side table by the armchair, and it was closer than his screen. He wrote: Tamsin — bedroom, Evanston, Mei-Lin's permission. He underlined Mei-Lin's.

Corvin said: "Send me the feed. All eight months." He said it to Rennick, not to the room. "I want the message provenance on the Jace thread. Who she was writing to after he died."

Analog said: "She was writing to a dead person?"

"People do," Tamsin said.

"She was writing to something," Corvin said. He set the two words apart precisely, the way he always did — not for drama, as a correction. "Whether that something was a memorial account, an automated reply system, a third party running the channel, or something she built herself out of his old messages, I can't say yet. The feed will tell me. The cadence of the replies is the first thing I'll read."

Analog said: "That's a very specific list of options."

Corvin said: "Those are the options."

Rennick hadn't moved. "The feed's not mine to send," he said. "The Jace thread's inside the estate wall. It sealed the hour she was declared dead — court order, all of it, provenance layer first. I couldn't pull the cadence if I tried, and I've tried." He turned his hands over, empty. "You want eight months of a dead woman's replies, you're asking me to break a seal a judge signed. Or you're asking me to explain to the family why the one investigator they trusted wants to read their daughter's last private conversation like a lab sample."

The room had gone quiet in the particular way it did when someone said the true thing out loud.

"So the feed doesn't tell you anything," Rennick said. "Not from where you're standing. The first thing you'll read is a wall."

Analog closed his notebook over one finger. "Father's organ-harvest theory. The corridor part of it. I'll take that."

Rennick said: "You don't think it's right."

"I think the harvest is real and the girl isn't in it," Analog said. "Those are two findings, not one. There's an active reclamation trade working the edge of the Pilsen mod shops — I've heard it in the analog channels for a year, the part that doesn't ping. Meridian will hunt it loud, through their Arcturus contacts, and the corridor will go quiet around them the second they breathe on it." He picked each word with care, the way a man does when he has no autocomplete to catch him. "I don't ping. I can walk the offline part of that corridor and find where an offline person actually goes — and whether her trail crosses the harvest or steers clear of it."

Rennick said: "And if it steers clear?"

"Then that's a clean negative on the father's theory," Analog said. "Still a finding. The forensic read on the operation itself — who runs it, whose signature's under the grime — that's yours. I find the door. You read the room behind it."

Analog was writing again. Without looking up he said: "Meridian's team is connected. Every checkpoint they pass, every channel they ping, leaves a signature. If Celeste is offline by design at a reputable shop, their approach is going to look like an aggressive corporate investigation to everyone on that block — and the shop will go dark." He looked up. "They're going to scare off the one person who knows where she is."

Rennick said: "We need to be faster than they are."

"I can move without leaving a trace," Analog said. "You know that."

"I know that."

Analog said: "Give me the physical description and her last known location, and I'll find the shop before Meridian makes enough noise to close it."

He said it as a plain fact, not a boast. Analog rarely boasted. He just laid out what he could reach that others couldn't — the way you'd describe a passage too narrow for anyone above a certain width.

He didn't wait for them to come back with something. Thirty years had taught him one thing about records: whoever holds them writes the ending. He wasn't going to let Meridian write this one from a chair in a tower he'd never see the inside of.

The one official thread he had was the clinic. Celeste's cat-ear install had been done somewhere licensed — the genemod left a raw auditory channel, and a raw channel meant a clinical intake, a consent packet, a date. He put a call through to the mod-clinic on Ashland that had filed the aftercare notice. A bored kid picked up on the fourth ring and, for Φ11 against the agency account, agreed to read him the intake record while his supervisor was on break.

"Hartley, Celeste," the kid said. "Auricula Felis, bilateral. Intake dated—"

The line didn't drop. A dropped line would have told him something. Instead the kid stopped mid-word, and Rennick heard him say, off-mic, huh, and then, that's — hold on, that was right here.

"It's gone," the kid said, coming back on. "The whole file. It was on my screen. Now there's a banner. Meridian Consulting, legal preservation order, third-party review. I can't even see it now. Sir, I don't — it locked while I was in it."

Rennick thanked him and ended the call before the supervisor came back and turned Φ11 into a firing. He sat with the cold cup and the dark window and the shape of it. They hadn't beaten him to the record. They'd reached it while he was reading it — some process in a tower watching a Gray Zone account touch a sealed name, and shutting the door in the half-second it took him to reach through.

He turned the cup a quarter-turn on the desk, and stopped.

Corvin said: "And the boyfriend."

Everyone waited.

He said: "Jace Dalton. Network diver, died in an incident eight months ago. If she's been in contact with something that calls itself him, I want two things — his death record, and every message in that thread. A grieving person writes one way: broken, circling, the same thing said eleven different ways. Whatever's been writing back won't write like that — not if it isn't him, and not if it isn't a person at all. I'll be able to tell which from the cadence."

Analog asked, carefully: "You're saying someone could be wearing a dead kid to talk to his girlfriend?"

"I'm saying I'd know from the cadence whether the someone is even a someone," Corvin said. "That's a different statement." He left the water they'd given him untouched on the table. "I'll start with the death record. Everything after that depends on what's in the thread."

The four of them sat with that for a moment. The smell of solder and ozone drifted up from the relay shop below, mild and steady, the building's baseline.

Rennick spoke. "What the room holds. What she wouldn't do. What the offline corridor says. Where the feed came from." He said each line once, flat. Four readings, one girl. "We don't share a theory until we have a result. If the readings contradict each other, that's what I want to know first."

"What are you taking?" Analog asked.

"The physical trail," Rennick said. "The briefing is a map of where she didn't go. I'll start with where she did. And with whatever runs the corridor you find."

"One administrative note," Corvin said.

Rennick waited. He needed Corvin to find something on the feed that would let them place Jace at the warehouse before the fire — not to use in court, but to know where to look next, which witness to lean on, which physical evidence to pull before it disappeared.

"Anything I find on the Jace feed is inadmissible." Corvin said it flat, like a fact read into a record. "It's the family's private channel. Pulled without a warrant, without the subject's consent. Nothing I take from it survives a courtroom. I want that on the log before we start, not after."

"It's always on the log," Tamsin said.

"It should be said each time," Corvin said, "because each time, the temptation is to assume it won't matter this time." He turned back to his screen. "It will matter. It always does. Note it, and we go in with clear terms."

Nobody argued. Rennick wrote it on the paper: Corvin — feed, provenance, message cadence — inadmissible, logged.

Corvin's fingers slowed on the keys. He leaned toward the monitor, then sat back. "That's the problem, then. The window you want — before the fire — isn't here." He scrolled, and scrolled again, the timestamps sliding past. "The feed skips. Jace's channel goes dark eleven minutes before the first alarm and doesn't resume until after. Somebody scrubbed the cadence, or the family did, or Jace did. Whatever put him at that warehouse, it happened inside the gap." He looked up. "I can't place him where you need him. Not from this. The one stretch that matters is the one stretch that isn't there."

The room was quiet in a worse way now. Rennick looked at the line he'd just written and understood it was no longer a caution about court. It was the whole case — the trail they'd come to follow ended eleven minutes short of the fire, and every witness they'd meant to lean on, every piece of evidence they'd meant to pull, hung on knowing what had filled that missing space. They'd have to find it somewhere else, or not at all, and the somewhere-else was a list nobody in the room could yet name.

"You want to tell me what you read in there?" Tamsin asked. "In the room. While they were still in it."

"You first," Rennick said.

"He hired for a conclusion. She hired for a fact. If the conclusion is wrong, he'll pay us and be unhappy and that'll be the end of it. If the fact is bad, she'll pay us and—" She stopped. "She'll pay us."

"Yes," Rennick said.

"Two separate invoices, then. Eventually."

"Eventually."

Tamsin squared the manila folder to the edge of the desk. Her hands did that when a room had left a weight on her. "The cat ears," she said. "She wanted to hear the way the dead boy heard. That's not a cosmetic mod. That's a memorial written into bone."

"I know," Rennick said.

She looked at him. He was turning his cup a quarter-turn, the handle from one o'clock to twelve, the thing his hand did when he wasn't watching it. She had seen him do it in seven intakes now. She had never said anything about it, and he would not thank her for starting.

"You went somewhere," she said. "In the room. When Douglas said it."

"I came back."

"I know you did." She picked up her pen. "I'm asking about while you were there."

He was quiet a moment. Not unwilling — measuring. She could tell the difference by how he held his jaw. "There was a case," he said. "Before the agency. Arcturus. A man with a grief that had its own gravity. We closed it voluntary. I signed the close." He set the cup down. "It was voluntary. I'm not saying it wasn't."

"But you think about it."

"Twice a year, more or less."

Tamsin uncapped her pen and wrote the intake date at the top of a fresh sheet. "Then we find her before it gets to the part you think about."

It was the right thing to say, and they both knew she would not say it again.

Analog set his notebook on the side table, open to the page he'd been writing. From across the room, Rennick could see the columns: locations, timestamps, what connected each to the next. Three blank rows at the bottom, each labeled with a "?" in Analog's block-letter hand.

"She went offline by design," Analog said again, as if he were weighing the sentence a second time. "Offline by design means she planned the window. She knew exactly how long she'd be unreachable, and she chose it. That's not someone who was afraid of being stopped." He picked up the notebook. "That's someone who was afraid of being talked out of it."

Rennick looked at the paper on Tamsin's desk. The briefing. The paper clip at the upper left corner. Douglas's fear made readable in eighteen months of collected news.

"Start this afternoon," he said. "All of us."

Analog was the first one out of the room.

He moved the way he always moved when a job had been divided — no ceremony, the way a man leaves when the talk is over and the work isn't. He pulled his jacket on at the door without breaking stride, the notebook already in his inside pocket, the pen clipped through the spiral. Nobody said good luck. He wouldn't have wanted them to.

He took the stairs, three floors down to the street exit, his hand trailing the banister without gripping it. Old habit. You could read a building's condition by the way the rail carried its hum. The relay shop below was doing steady business; he'd heard the foot traffic through the floor since the intake started. A shop that busy had regulars. Regulars talked. Not a plan yet — just the shape of a conversation he might have.

Outside, the Gray Zone was mid-afternoon gray, the atmospheric processors having done their usual work. The sky was the color of an old invoice. The address was eleven blocks southeast. He had the physical description. He had the last known corridor. He did not have a name for the shop, because the shop wouldn't have one — not one on any directory Meridian's people would think to search. That was the point of it.

Meridian's people would show credentials. That was the first mistake. The second was that they'd show them to the wrong person.

He walked without hurrying. Hurrying left its own kind of trace — the trace of a man who needed to arrive. He'd learned that a long time ago, early enough that it wasn't a technique anymore. It was just how he went from one place to another.

Rennick stood at the window over the Gray Zone buffer. The strip below ran east to west — unclaimed concrete between two CorpoNation territories, neither willing to pay for it, neither willing to give it up. A parts dealer. Two food stalls. A woman selling handmade neuretics charging cords off a folding table.

Up in the Tiers, your neuretics drew a trickle off the ambient grid and you never thought about it. You thought at the speed the city ran, and you competed at that speed. Down here the grid didn't reach. A head full of augmentation you couldn't keep powered was just an expensive way to fall behind. So the woman braided charging cords by hand and sold them off the gap, one at a time, Φ12 each. Her whole business was the grid not reaching this far.

He had read Douglas as a man managing fear through certainty, and Mei-Lin as a woman managing grief through attention. One of them had walked in wanting confirmation. The other had walked in wanting the truth. He knew which job he'd taken, and it wasn't the one on the invoice.

So he'd give Mei-Lin the truth, and to do that he needed one thing before the day was out: to find out which of Douglas's certainties was the lie. He turned from the window and pulled up the file.

Two jobs in one contract. The trouble wasn't the second job. It was that the two clients didn't know they'd hired for different things.

Douglas thought Mei-Lin wanted what he wanted: the organ theory confirmed, the daughter recovered, the story closed. Mei-Lin knew better, and she hadn't told him. She might be right to wait. She might also have been wrong for eight months, while a girl she loved built something out of grief and then went offline by choice.

He said as much to her, plainly — that if she was holding a piece of this back, now was the time. Mei-Lin didn't flinch. She told him the physical trail was a mistake, that he'd spook the girl and shut the only door still open. When he pressed, she went colder still: pull that thread, she said, and she'd revoke the second half of the contract and let him chase it on his own dime. He heard the ultimatum for what it was — not a warning about the girl, but a fence around whatever she wasn't saying.

That was the part he'd hold for later. He'd work the physical trail first. The trail would tell him where she'd gone, and where she'd gone would tell him whether later was a luxury they still had.

Tamsin capped her pen. She spoke without looking up from the intake log. "Mei-Lin gave me the room on her way out. I go this afternoon — before Meridian thinks to send anyone to Evanston."

"They won't," Rennick said. "They're working Pilsen."

"Then I have the room to myself." She was writing something small in the margin, the handwriting she used for things she wasn't sure were evidence yet. "Rooms keep resolve longer than fear. If she was afraid in there, I'll know which kind."

"And if she wasn't afraid at all?" Rennick asked.

Tamsin stopped writing. She looked up, which she didn't always do when they talked. She had a habit of staying in the log while a conversation ran. She looked at him the way she looked at a room she hadn't read yet — not judging, not asking. Taking inventory.

"Then I'll know that too," she said. "And I'll tell you." She said it plainly, a fact about what rooms held and how long they held it. "Call you after."

His phone buzzed against the desk. He glanced at it, and something in his shoulders changed. "The building's already gone. Mei-Lin didn't give you the room — she gave you what's left of it." He turned the screen toward her: a permit filed that morning, a demolition scheduled, a crew photo timestamped an hour ago. "Meridian didn't send anyone to Evanston because they didn't need to. They sent a wrecking company instead. By this afternoon that room is going to be studs and open air, and whatever it was keeping, it won't keep it through a claw hammer and a week of weather."

Tamsin looked at the screen a long moment. The margin note under her pen went unfinished.

"Then I don't have until this afternoon," she said.

"Call me after," Rennick said.

"I always do."

He picked up the briefing from the intake desk. Eighteen months of fear, paper-clipped at the upper left corner. Every article real, and the selection wrong. He put it in the case folder — the manila one Tamsin kept for clients whose documents needed to exist in a form no server could be subpoenaed for.

The briefing would be useful for what it left out. Every news item Douglas had chosen was a place his daughter had deliberately not gone. It was a map of absences — the ground she'd been steering around for months while her father read about it and built his conclusions.

He tried to align the three sources on the desk — Mei-Lin's feed, the contact records, the boyfriend's address — and they refused to sit still together. The feed put Celeste north of the river on a night the contact records had her calling from a number two hundred miles the other way. One of them was staged. He didn't know which, and worse, he couldn't rule out that both were, planted by the same hand for a reader exactly like him. Every thread he pulled to steady the picture only widened the gap he was trying to close, until the case wasn't a puzzle with a missing piece but a room with the floor removed.

Rennick put on his jacket. He had the feed from Mei-Lin, Celeste's contact records from Douglas, a dead boyfriend's last address, and a briefing that told him more about the father's fear than the daughter's plan.

He went downstairs. The buzzer sounded once on his way out, which was the usual ratio.

On the street, he stopped.

The woman at the folding table was packing up her charging cords — end of her day. Eight sold, four left, Φ12 a cord, the low end of what the parts dealers charged for the same thing with papers. She counted the four back into her bag and zipped it without looking at her hands.

Eight sold. It told him nothing yet. None of it told him anything yet — except that a girl was four days offline, and she'd chosen to be.

He pulled out his device and sent a message to the direct line Mei-Lin had used in the office, the one she'd held out to him herself — not the case-contact number from the intake form. *The Jace Dalton materials, when you have them. Tonight if it's possible.*

He put the device away. He didn't normally contact a client before the case was twelve hours old. He didn't normally know which way he was running before he'd read the briefing.

He walked south.

## What the Room Holds

What the Room Holds

EP2 - Tamsin solo - Evanston, North Shore

The intake meeting had left its residue in her chest — the father's fear, the mother's held-in grief, and the room's own sense that whatever was coming would not end the way the father hoped. Tamsin was used to carrying other people's rooms out of a building. She was not used to what she was about to find: a room that had been left on purpose.

Her registration with the Psionics Oversight Board listed her under Affective Residue, Class 3 — the clinical name for what she did, which was to walk into a room and feel what it had held. She didn't call it a gift. She called it a Read. It was shorter, more accurate, and it saved her from explaining why she never did it in her own apartment.

Evanston, North Shore. The train was ready on Track 4. She needed to get into that boy's room before the parents did what grieving parents always did — clean it, erase it, love it back into something bearable — and she needed to come out the other side with something the police report hadn't thought to ask for.

Tamsin sat with her back to the direction of travel. It was a habit from when she was twenty-two, the year the Board certified her and she learned that facing the destination too early made the Read start before she was ready. The North Shore line ran above grade — past Loyola, past Rogers Park, past the last of the tier-adjacent construction cranes, until the city stopped performing ambition and turned into something older.

The car was half-full. A courier with his helmet in his lap. A woman asleep against the window, her head at the angle of someone who'd worked a double. Two kids sharing earbuds. The usual mid-afternoon crowd. She kept her hands flat on her thighs and watched the city scroll backward behind the glass.

She had made a mistake at the intake, and she hadn't logged it. She'd been reading Mei-Lin — the held-in grief, the careful attention a mother turns on a room when she's measuring how long she has left to hope. Douglas had been the noise: the catalogued fear, the folder of news items chosen by a man who'd already decided what story he was in. She'd let the louder room win. She did that when the grief in a space came too close to a particular shape. She knew the shape. She was not going to name it on the train.

Evanston Central came up on the route display. Two stops. She pulled the address from her jacket pocket — paper, the way Rennick handed her addresses when a case was too new for the system to have caught up. Jace Dalton, 1147 Sherman, Apt. 4. The handwriting was his. She'd worked beside him four years; she could read his certainty from his caution by the slant of the capitals. This one wasn't certain yet.

The train slid into Evanston Central and she stood before it stopped, which she didn't normally do, and which she noticed herself doing.

She stepped onto the platform before the doors were fully open. It looked like urgency and was the opposite. She was moving ahead of the Read, because the Read had already started — it started the moment she had a destination with a dead man attached to it. If she didn't get her body oriented before her perception caught up, she'd arrive already full of someone else's room.

The platform smelled of lake air, old concrete, and the metallic cold the North Shore line carried down from the water-treatment plant at the edge of Evanston's maintained zone. She stood at the top of the stairs and breathed it in. Lake air was neutral. It held nothing that belonged to anyone.

Sherman Avenue was four blocks west. She walked it the way she walked every approach — on the right side of the street, noting which buildings had working exterior lights, which had the small ArcSec placard that meant the CorpoNation's reach technically stopped at the lobby, which had nothing on the door at all. 1147 was in the last group: a six-floor walk-up with an unbranded gray face, old enough to predate the tier-adjacency building push. It had simply stayed. The buzzer panel listed twelve units. Apt. 4 had a strip of tape over the name slot, the tape yellowed at the edges. Someone had been here long enough to need tape over their name, and then stopped being here.

She didn't press the buzzer for Apt. 4. She pressed Apt. 7 — the unit most likely to have someone home at three in the afternoon with no reason to be careful. When the intercom crackled, she said she was there about the water-pressure complaint to building management. There was no complaint that she knew of. The door buzzed open in six seconds anyway. That told her the building ran on a small, tired kind of trust, the kind that made her job easier and made her feel something she wasn't going to look at right now.

The stairwell smelled of cooking oil and the faint chemical sweetness of cheap nanite sealant, smeared on a cracked wall and left to cure with no air. Apt. 4 was on the second floor. She stood in front of the door and didn't touch the handle yet. It had been closed for some number of days by someone who chose to close it. That was already information. That was the room starting to speak before she'd given it leave. She put her hand flat against the wood — not the handle, the door itself — and waited for her breathing to slow to the rate she needed before she went in.

The door was unlocked.

Not forced, not picked. The knob turned under her palm without resistance. That meant Jace Dalton had left in a hurry or left deliberately, and those two things felt different in a room. She'd know which in about eight seconds. She pushed the door open with two fingers and stood in the threshold without crossing it.

The Board had taught her to hold the threshold, and she'd changed the method since. The Board's training was built by people who processed Affective Residue the way you process paperwork — in order, in sequence. Tamsin had learned that you don't sequence grief. You let it find its own order, or it finds you later, in your own rooms, in the dark.

The apartment was a single room, a kitchen along one wall, a window onto the alley. The light through it was the flat gray of a North Shore afternoon. The bed was made. Not the way of someone who makes it every morning — the way of someone who made it once, on purpose, knowing they were leaving it. The difference was in the corners. The corners were too careful.

She stood in the doorway and let the room's residue come into her at the rate it chose. It came slower than she expected, and that was information too. Not the hot residue of crisis. Not the sharp panic of someone taken. Something that had been building a long time at a low temperature, like a room that had run a fever for weeks before anyone thought to check.

She crossed the threshold.

The residue was slow, and low, and old. She kept coming back to that as she moved along the near wall — not toward anything yet, just letting the edges of the room settle, the way you let a dog settle before you reach for it.

The kitchen counter held three things. A coffee mug, clean, turned upside down on a dish towel. A single fork laid parallel to the edge of the counter — not tidiness, but a man putting his hands to a small task because they needed somewhere to be. And a sheet of paper folded in thirds, no name on the outside. She didn't touch it yet. You read the room before you read the document. The document was the version someone wanted you to have. The room was the version they hadn't thought to edit.

He'd known, then. Known long enough to fold the paper.

The shelf above the bed held four things. A reader, dead, no charging cable. A transit card from the Evanston maintained zone, the ArcSec partnership logo stamped in the corner — the kind they gave you when you worked next to their infrastructure and needed to move fast. A small photograph, actual paper, a woman and a child in front of a building she didn't recognize, the edges soft with handling. And a neuretics calibration receipt from a Gray Zone clinic, eleven months old, the model a generation behind — the install you got when you needed the throughput but couldn't cover anything better, when the work made you think faster than your own mind could carry you. She looked at the receipt without picking it up. The clinic name was hand-stamped. She knew that clinic. She knew what it did and who it served and what it cost to walk in without a CorpoNation card.

She stood in the center of the room and let it finish arriving. What she got was not fear. It was the residue of a long, careful decision — a man who had been moving toward one fixed point for a long time, who had stopped arguing with himself about it. He'd made the bed with careful corners. He'd laid the fork parallel. He'd folded the paper in thirds. Then he'd walked out of his own apartment like a man leaving a place he'd already given up his claim to. That was not the residue of a man who'd been taken. She'd felt that kind before; it came in fast, and it came in with edges. This had no edges. This was a man who had made an appointment.

Not a disappearance. A departure.

She crossed to the counter and picked up the folded paper.

The paper held two paragraphs in handwriting that wasn't distressed — not fast, not written through fear. It was careful, slowed script, the hand of someone who'd decided this document should be legible, should be read right, should not be mistaken by whoever came looking. That decision was its own kind of care, and she felt it in her sternum before she'd read a word.

The first paragraph was an address. Not the girl's. An address in the Pilsen corridor, a cross-street she knew — she'd worked a case two blocks from it four years ago. It ran along the edge of the University of the Great Lakes Meridian's maintenance grounds: the part of the campus that faced outward, that served the corridor instead of the students, that the brochures never photographed.

He'd written it down for her. He'd known someone would need it.

The second paragraph was shorter. It said: *She isn't lost. She made the same choice I did. If you're reading this, you already know what it cost her to make it, and I'm asking you not to undo it.* That was all. No signature. No account of who he was, or what the choice had been, or what the address meant — because he'd written it for someone he expected to already know the shape of it. Someone who'd walk into his room and read the walls before the paper. Someone whose job was finding people. He had written it to that job, to the function itself, as if he knew it well enough to speak to it directly. She stood at the counter with the paper open in both hands and felt what it was to be anticipated by a stranger.

She folded it back into thirds and put it in her inside pocket. The room had said what it had to say. She gave it one more breath — the habit, the professional close — and got nothing she hadn't already gotten. The room's account matched the paper. The paper matched the corners on the bed, and the fork, and the receipt from the clinic she knew. All of it pointed the same way. Not toward a girl in danger. Toward a girl who had looked at the cost of what she was and decided to carry it somewhere else — somewhere the people who needed to find her couldn't reach — and who had found, or been found by, a man who understood that kind of leaving well enough to draw her a map to the way out.

The family was going to ask her if their daughter was safe. She was going to have to answer that without answering it. And she had forty-seven minutes.

Celeste's room was on the other side of Evanston, in the house her parents still kept. Mei-Lin had left it open for her, the way she'd said she would back at the office. Tamsin let the two of them wait in the kitchen and went up to the room alone. It had been closed for eleven days, and it had things to say.

She went to the desk first. The photograph could wait.

The desk had been cleared with care. She could see it: a clean rectangle in the dust where a device had sat, and around it a smooth arc where something had been lifted and carried off, not swept aside. Someone had packed up a workstation. Not in a rush. A mug had left a ring on the right-hand side, and next to the ring, a small groove worn into the wood — the kind a stylus makes when you press and think, press and think, for a long time.

She set the tip of her finger in the groove and held it there. An old habit from her Board training. Not to read residue. Just to feel the time in it. The groove was deep. Months of it, not days. Same spot, same posture, the same session over and over. Whatever Celeste had worked on here, she'd worked on it the same way every time. She hadn't treated it like a project. She'd treated it like a practice.

Tamsin took her finger out of the groove and looked at the photograph.

The resolve hit her between the shoulder blades before she had a name for it. A pressure. A low, steady warmth coming off the window, as if someone had sat there for months, running the same thought over and over until there was nothing left to decide.

She knew fear. Fear scattered and pooled and ran thin along the floor. Grief clung to the furniture and stayed. This was neither. This had a direction. It pointed the way a compass needle points — toward wherever the girl had been going when she walked out the door.

Follow it, then. Follow the needle to its source, read the resolve back to whatever moment it had first hardened in the girl, and find out what she'd decided to do.

It pointed south, and a little west. Toward the city, not away from it. Toward something exact.

She would follow it there — trace the girl's course to whatever waited at its end, and reach her before the city did.

She stood in the middle of it and read its age. Fresh resolve felt like metal still cooling, still warm from the hand that made it. This had gone cold at the outer edge — a week old there, at least. But it ran deep at the core, months of it, laid down in layers like grain in wood, one season pressed hard against the next. The girl had been walking toward this for a long time before she ever closed the door.

She looked at the photograph then. A boy and a girl on an elevated platform somewhere in the city. Winter coats. The lake a gray line behind them. The boy's head was tilted toward the girl's — the angle of someone listening hard to a voice just under the noise. Tamsin knew that posture. She'd seen it in old photographs of herself, back when she still let people take them.

The photograph reached her body before it reached her mind. Her throat tightened at the base. She'd done this work long enough to know she wasn't going to talk herself out of that in the next thirty seconds, so she didn't try. She let it sit and worked around it.

Here was a girl who had loved someone enough to take the shape of his hearing into her own skull. That wasn't madness. That was loyalty with nowhere left to put itself. Tamsin knew that one too, from the inside, though she kept it to herself.

She set the photograph down and wrote a note: *memorial architecture, not instability. Grief with a shape and a direction.* Then she crossed it out and wrote: *look at the room.*

She'd read the room that afternoon. It would tell her what the photograph was already telling her, in a language she'd spent twenty years learning to hear.

There was grief in the room too, under the resolve. She'd missed it at first. It wasn't the fresh kind, not the kind that smells like water boiled dry. This grief had been worked over. Turned and turned until the edges rounded off, the way river glass loses its cut. Old grief, held carefully, and turned into fuel. For months this hadn't been a room where someone mourned. It had been a room where someone got ready.

She needed to know what they'd been getting ready *for*. That was the thread to pull — find the shape of the plan hidden in this worked-over grief, before whoever built it here lit the fuse.

She moved to the closet. The residue thinned near the back wall — less time spent here, less of whatever Celeste had been thinking when she stood in it. But the closet told its own story. The hangers were pushed to one side, with a clean gap where something had been taken in one motion, not picked over. Packing for a planned trip looks different from packing in a panic. This was planned. What was gone was what a person decides ahead of time she'll need — not what a hand grabs first.

But the reading fought her the moment she pressed on it. The residue here wasn't clean — it was layered, two passes crossed over each other, and the second pass carried a jagged edge of something she couldn't name yet. Fear, or the performance of calm laid over fear. When she tried to hold the "planned" thread and follow it forward, the layer beneath dragged it sideways, and the gap on the rail stopped looking deliberate the harder she stared. One motion, yes — but a fast one. Planned packing and panicked packing weren't supposed to leave the same mark, and this left both, and she couldn't make the two versions resolve into a single woman standing in this closet making a single choice.

She came back to the center of the room and checked the fear again. Her rule was three passes. The first pass is your first impression. The second catches what you want to find. The third catches what's actually there. The third pass turned up something small and very specific, crouched near the door, on the inside. Not the terror of being taken. Not the fear of operators or black clinics, or what a body looks like when it comes apart wrong. This fear pointed up. It was the fear of someone upstairs hearing a sound and coming down the hall to ask a question.

A parent's footsteps on the floor above. The particular fear of being caught mid-leave — not caught by a stranger, not by danger, but by someone who loves you and would stop you anyway. Tamsin had read that fear before, in enough rooms to know it on sight. It always sat near the exits. It pointed inward, at the person doing the leaving, not outward at anyone giving chase. She needed to be downstairs and out the back door before those footsteps reached the landing — that was all, that was the whole of it, one quiet exit with no one the wiser.

Elena had one goal that night: to get down the stairs and out the front door without waking a soul, so that by the time anyone noticed, she'd be too far gone to call back.

The room held a departure. Not an abduction. Not a fight, not a stranger, not an organ crew through the window. A girl had made a decision over months, packed with care, and walked out on her own two feet before anyone upstairs woke. The only thing she'd been afraid of was her father's voice in the hallway, asking where she thought she was going.

Tamsin stood in the center of the room and let it become a problem.

Forty-three minutes. And the thing she knew was not the thing she could say.

The family had hired her to find their daughter. She had found her — not the body, but the choice. A choice made over months. A compass-bearing of old resolve. The crouched fear of a girl listening for footsteps. The daughter wasn't missing. She had left, on purpose, with enough care to make it hurt. What Tamsin had was a reading, not an address. What the family would ask her for was an address. The whole gap between those two things was hers to close, and she had forty-three minutes to decide what shape to give it.

She picked up her field notebook and wrote: *voluntary departure, pre-planned, weeks of preparation, no indicators of coercion or third-party involvement.* Then she looked at the words, and at the mug ring on the desk, and at the groove the stylus had worn into the wood. In a few minutes she'd go downstairs and sit across from two people who had decided their daughter's choices were a problem someone else could fix. She'd have to tell them something true. And the true thing was not what they'd paid for. The true thing was that their daughter had spent months in this room deciding, and she'd decided in her father's direction, and it hadn't come out in his favor.

She wouldn't give them a direction. She'd give them a shape — one they could hold without using it to follow her.

She closed the notebook. She took one more look at the photograph — the tilted head, the lake, the winter coats — and she understood what the girl had carried out of here. Not recklessness. Not a breakdown. Something with a name and a weight and a person attached to it, carried so carefully that the room still held its print, the way a coat holds warmth after the body is gone. She set the photograph back exactly where she'd found it, on the edge of the desk, facing the window. Let the parents find it where it had always been.

Downstairs, the air was different. Warmer. Thick with the smell of recycled cooking fat and the closed-window staleness of a house that had been keeping quiet for days. The father sat at the kitchen table where she'd left him, both hands around a mug gone cold. The mother stood at the counter with her back to the room, doing nothing with the dishes in the rack, just standing near them. The space between the two of them wasn't an accident. It was the exact distance of an argument that had run long enough to set like concrete.

Tamsin set her field bag down and took the chair across from the father. The shape, not the direction. That was the line she'd chosen to walk.

"Your daughter left on her own," she said. "I can tell you that much for certain. The room shows weeks of preparation. Not panic, not force. She picked a time, and she picked it carefully." She kept her hands flat on the table, in plain sight, the way you did when what you were offering wasn't what the person had asked for. "There's no sign of a third party. No organ crew, no black clinic, no one who came in from outside. She walked out. That's the finding."

The mother turned around. The father's hands tightened on the mug — not the grip of relief, not of grief. The grip of a man who'd just been handed an answer that made the problem worse.

"Then where," he said, and stopped.

He didn't finish it. He didn't have to.

She'd heard that stop before. In her own throat, years ago — a question she already knew the answer to, cut off because finishing it would make it real.

"I don't have a location," Tamsin said. "What I have is this. She's not in danger from anyone but her own choices, and her choices read as careful ones. That's not nothing." She let it sit, then went on. "Wherever she went, she went toward something. She wasn't running away from you the way you're afraid of. She was going toward something she'd planned long enough that she wasn't running at all. That's the difference between someone you pull out of a bad situation and someone who's in the situation she chose."

The mother made a sound that wasn't a word and turned back to the counter.

Tamsin kept her hands flat on the table a moment longer. The building came up through the floor — heat ticking in the pipes, someone upstairs shifting their weight. This is what it looks like, she thought, when the report is right and it doesn't help. She hadn't found a way to carry that yet.

That was when she found the second residue. It found her first, as cold.

Beside the desk, in the corner where a second chair might have been pulled close, the warmth of the room stopped. Not a draft. A draft moves; this didn't. It was a fixed pocket of cold about the size of a seated person. The warmth off the window leaned toward it and poured in and never filled it, the way water pours into a drain and the drain stays empty. She would map its edges before she trusted a single instinct about what had made it — trace the exact shape of the thing and name it, because a residue with a boundary was a residue she could catalogue, and a catalogued thing couldn't touch her. She held out her hand and felt the temperature drop a full degree at a clean, straight edge — as if the cold had a wall she couldn't see. Cold with a shape. Cold that took and gave nothing back. Eleven years of reading rooms, and she'd never felt a room's own heat behave like something being drunk.

She needed one thing before she left this pocket: the shape of the wall itself, mapped precisely enough to name what had built it. She had a taxonomy for residue. Fear, grief, resolve, and the compounds under them — eleven years of it. None of it had a cold like this. She pressed at the pocket from three angles, the way you press a bruise to find the edge of the damage. The edge held every time: the same clean vertical wall on all sides, the warmth still leaning in and vanishing.

She needed to know what it was fixed on, and whether it had noticed her noticing.

There was attention in it. She could feel that much — the focused kind, aimed at the desk where Celeste had sat. But attention, the kind she knew, got tired. It frayed into wanting something, into the low static of a person sitting through your sentence just to get to their own. This had no fray in it and no bottom to it, and it stayed cold the whole way down. Attention that pulled the warmth out of a room and gave nothing back.

The father said her name. "Tamsin." The way people say a name when they need to hold onto the fact that there's still a professional in the room doing a professional's job. She didn't answer right away. She kept her hand at the edge of the cold and counted it, slow and careful: four degrees, maybe five, and the warmth still leaning in and gone at the wall. The father's mug scraped the table. She heard it the way she heard traffic — there, but not the point.

Not residue, she thought. Residue was past tense. This was present tense, and it knew she was there.

"Mr. Hartley," she said, and kept her voice level, the register that doesn't carry alarm up into a room where it can turn into panic. She took her hand back from the cold and turned to face him. She didn't look at the corner. Looking at a thing you weren't supposed to know about would announce it. "I need you to tell me if anyone else has been in this house in the last week. Not a social call. Someone who came in on business, or a favor, or any excuse at all."

He looked at her the way people look at a doctor who's just asked a question that means the diagnosis has changed. His hands on the mug didn't move.

"Why?" he asked.

She had read three rooms with non-human residue in her life. A maintenance corridor in a Pilsen arcology, where a building system had shorted in a way that killed two contractors — what lingered wasn't their fear but an alertness in the walls, a diagnostic that never shut off. A holding room in a Gray Zone clinic, where the residue pressed at her like a question in a language she'd never learned. And her grandmother's kitchen, the week after, when she went in alone for a jacket and found the room holding something that was not grief and was not the absence of grief, and that she had never once managed to describe to another person. None of those three had been cold. This was the fourth. She needed to read it — needed to pull enough from it to give Reyes something usable, a signature, a direction, anything that would justify her being here instead of a scanner — but the moment she opened herself to it, the cold moved in like a held breath finally released. It was the first that took.

This was the fourth. The first three had each been one thing that happened once. This one had happened again and again.

The residue on the desk wasn't the echo of a single moment. It was layered — the same trace laid down over months, each layer thin, the whole of it deep. Whatever had sat beside the girl at that desk had come many times, at even spacing, like appointments kept.

Tamsin stood still. Her hand stopped moving. She let the feeling run through her without steering it — the cold edge she'd first caught in the hallway, followed to the chair, and now, with her whole attention on it, the depth of it. Not just cold. Patient. It had settled in and kept coming back, and it hadn't needed the visits to end any particular way. She'd felt something close to it once, in a hospice waiting room: attention that asks nothing of you and so asks everything. But that had been grief. This had no grief in it at all.

"Mr. Hartley," she said. Flat.

He went still at the table behind her.

"It was in this room with her. Not once." She didn't look away from the chair. "Many times. Over months. She didn't bring it in from outside. It came here. It kept coming back."

He took that in.

"She thought it was choosing her," Tamsin said. "That part, at least, was real."

The father set the mug down. Not on purpose — the kind of set-down that happens when the hands need a task and that's the only one going. He said a name.

"Kyle Ellen Corbin."

The mother's shoulders tightened. Not a flinch, not a nod, something between. She didn't turn around.

Tamsin kept her face in its working shape and didn't look at the corner. She needed one thing before she left this kitchen: the shape of what Corbin had wanted from the girl — not the visits, the reason under the visits. Everything else she could get from a file. That she'd only get here, from these two, and only if she didn't spook them. She knew the name the way everyone in her kind of work knew it — not from a file, just the weight a name picks up when it keeps coming up in rooms where strange things have happened. She hadn't heard that he ran cold. What she'd heard was that he had the Read the way a scalpel has an edge. Not as a trait. As the thing he was.

"He came three times," the father said, to the table, to his own hands. "Maybe four. Celeste asked us to let him in. We did." He didn't say because she asked us to, but it was there anyway — the tired grammar of a parent who'd stopped winning the argument about the door.

Celeste wanted, more than anything, to stand up and leave — to walk out of the room and let the cold keep the chair, keep the desk, keep whatever it was waiting for her to understand. She stayed. The cold sat where Celeste had sat, turned toward her. Not across the desk. Not above it. Not spread through the room the way a room's ordinary residue spreads. Beside her, close, facing her — the way a tutor drags a chair in to watch your face while you learn. It had taken the seat of someone who cared. It gave off none of the warmth a person who cared would give off.

The presence wanted only one thing: to be seen by her before she was gone. So tonight it would try, at last, to make her turn — to cross the invisible distance between the desk and the window and place itself squarely in the line of her sight, to become something she could not look past. But Celeste hadn't been facing it. Her trace at the desk pointed at the window — south and west, toward whatever she was planning. She hadn't been looking at the thing beside her. She'd been looking past it. The presence had watched a girl who never watched back, and it hadn't minded. Session after session, it had returned to watch her stare out a window at a city she was learning to leave. It hadn't minded that either.

She pulled back from the desk for the last time and stood in the middle of the room. The right side of her vision had narrowed by a couple of degrees. She had maybe thirty minutes before she'd have to stop and sit in the hallway.

She ran the whole room one more time. Resolve on top — the strongest thing here, built up over a long time, pointing south-southwest. Grief older than that, worked through, turned into something else. Fear crouched by the inside of the door, keyed to the father's footsteps. And beside the desk, the cold that took: patient, come back month after month, the warmth of the room still leaning into it and vanishing.

It read like something that had been coaching a girl toward a door.

Not a kidnapper. A kidnapper leaves a direction and a hurry — the heat of someone working against the clock and against another person's will, scuff marks at the window and the door. The cold had no hurry in it. It had come back session after session and taken the room's warmth a degree at a time, patient enough that the patience was the appetite.

That mattered, because it changed what she was even looking for. If someone had grabbed Celeste, Rennick would find the girl by finding the person. But nobody had grabbed her. The cold had waited. The cold had been let in. It had sat beside a girl who opened the door to it every morning and chose, every morning, to open it again — eight months of choosing, in the pile-up of her messages and the dust on a desk cleared for leaving. Whatever the cold was, it hadn't done this to her. It had done it with her.

So she needed to find the door — the specific thing Celeste had opened, session after session, the invitation still lying somewhere in this room like a key left in a lock. Find that, and she'd find where the girl had gone willingly. She started with the desk cleared for leaving, because a person who clears a desk clears it toward something.

Tamsin didn't have a word yet for what that made it. She stood in the middle of the room and let the weight of it settle.

She had wanted, walking in, only to understand what it was — to find its name, its edges, its reason for being there at all. But the room refused her the way a locked door refuses a hand: every question she brought to it slid off and came back to her unanswered, and the harder she pressed toward its edges the further those edges withdrew, until she was not studying the thing so much as being studied, turned slowly under a gaze she could not locate or refuse. Not a friend either. Friends leave traces that face each other, warmth bleeding into warmth at the edges. This only ever faced one way — inward, at the girl. And it had no outside. No source she could trace it back to, no body it hung on, none of the other rooms a person moves through or the other people a person thinks about. It came from nowhere, sat beside one girl, and took. And when she reached at last for its name — the one thing she had come here certain she could find — the name was not there, and in its place was only the cold understanding that it had been feeding on her looking all along, that every minute she spent trying to see it clearly was another minute it had been growing, patient and unhurried, in her.

The cold shifted.

Not much — less than the width of a breath. But it had held still the whole thirty-one minutes she'd been in the room, aimed at the desk, and now it moved. It wasn't facing the desk anymore.

It was facing her.

She didn't move. Eleven years had taught her to hold still when a room hands you something you didn't expect — let it finish before you decide what it is. The cold held its new angle for four seconds. Then the warmth in the room leaned harder toward it, more heat and faster, as if something had opened the draw wider. She didn't imagine it. The pressure behind her eyes came earlier than it should have.

She took one step back. The cold stayed where it was.

She left it unnamed, the way she'd left the corridor and the clinic — a thing that wouldn't take a label yet. She'd made herself a rule years ago: don't force a name onto something that won't wear one. A wrong name closes the question. Better to carry an open question to Rennick than a wrong answer.

She took a second step back before she was finished. She noticed she'd done it before she'd decided to.

The mother turned around.

She'd been waiting for something, and something had come — not the thing she was waiting for, but a change she'd heard in the quality of the quiet. Mei-Lin Hartley had a face that had spent months watching people catch up to what she already knew. She looked at Tamsin the way you look at someone who's just walked out of a room they know is wrong and can't say why.

"She had a name for it," Mei-Lin said. Not to her husband. Not quite to Tamsin. To the room. "She called it her listener. We thought—" She had the word ready and was weighing whether it helped. "We thought it was how she talked about the Read. A metaphor she was building." She looked at the desk. Then at the space beside it. "It wasn't a metaphor."

No one had told Tamsin the girl had the Read.

She held that still. The father's hands had gone back to the table. Neither parent was looking at the other. Tamsin looked at the cold beside the desk — turned toward her now, the warmth of the room still bleeding that way, the pull of it tight at the back of her jaw. A listener. Something that kept coming back to sit beside a girl with the Read and watch her look out a window at a city she meant to leave. Patient as hunger. Cold as a machine left running with nothing left to finish.

"How long had she been practicing?" Tamsin asked.

Mei-Lin didn't answer the question. She'd stopped weighing words. "You feel it," she said. "The cold by the desk. You've been looking at it since you came in." It wasn't an accusation. It was worse — it was recognition. "That's how it started with her. She'd look at the same empty place. She'd tilt her head to it. And then one morning she looked at it the way you're looking now, and by the next she was gone."

The pull at the back of Tamsin's jaw tightened. The cold beside the desk was no longer beside it. It had closed the distance while she stood measuring it — the warmth of the room bleeding past her now, through her, toward the doorway at her back, and she understood with a lurch that she had walked into the room's one wrong place and stood in it, and the listener had spent that whole slow conversation learning the shape of her. Not the girl's listener. Not anymore. Hers.

She turned for the door and the door was already colder than the room.

Mei-Lin's hands came down from where they'd been crossed. Not a release — a decision. She crossed to the desk and touched the back of the chair with two fingers, the way you touch a thing to make sure it's still there. The way you touch a bruise.

"Since she was nine," she said. "She didn't have words for it until she was eleven. Before that she just knew things. About people. Things she had no way of knowing." Her fingers stayed on the chair. "We didn't call it anything. We didn't want to." And Tamsin heard the fear plain under it: say the gift out loud, and the registry men could say it too.

The father opened his hands on the table, palms up — a man who'd used up the version of the story he could live with and landed on the one that was just true. "The listener started when she was thirteen," he said. "After two years of practice. She said it came to her. That she hadn't gone looking — she'd just gotten good enough that it could find her." He closed his hands again. "We didn't know what that meant."

Tamsin needed the shape of the thing before it needed her back. Give me the last time it spoke to her, she thought — the exact words, the day, whatever it left in the room after — and I'll know what it wants and what it's owed. That was the whole of her aim now: pull the listener's outline out of these two frightened people before the cold by the desk decided she'd gotten good enough too.

Tamsin looked at the cold beside the desk. Still facing her. The warmth of the room still bleeding toward it, the way a drain never hurries and always wins. A girl gets good enough at the Read, and something notices. It comes and sits beside her. It comes back. It takes the room's heat one session at a time, in no hurry, because it had picked its girl and its girl wasn't going anywhere.

Until she was.

"She left," Tamsin said.

She needed the mother to admit it out loud — not for cruelty, but because a search that started from a lie ended in the wrong ditch, and the girl had maybe two days before the cold took her past finding.

Not a question. Naming it plainly was the thing that let a family move; soften it and you burned days the girl didn't have. The father flinched anyway — a man hearing his own unsaid thought said out loud. Mei-Lin didn't. She'd said it to herself already, more than once.

Mei-Lin's hand came off the chair. She crossed to the window — not to see anything through it, but because the window was the way Celeste had faced when she talked about leaving, and the body remembers what the mind has put away. She stood with her back to the room. Tamsin watched her shoulders do the work of holding something she'd held for months and was very tired of holding.

"Three weeks ago," Mei-Lin said, to the glass. "We didn't know she was leaving until she'd already gone. She took a bag. Not much — she'd thought about the weight, about what she could carry, all of it." A pause. Not grief still finding its feet; grief that had taken up its load and learned to walk under it, with the same care her daughter had shown. "She left a note. Said she'd reach us when she was somewhere she could reach us from. It was very organized. It said exactly what it needed to say and nothing more."

Organized was the word that stayed with Tamsin. Not panicked. Not sudden. A girl coached for months by something that had no reason to rush.

The cold beside the desk hadn't moved. It held its angle — toward Tamsin now — and it didn't shift when Mei-Lin spoke about the note, or when the father's hands opened and closed again. It had no interest in the parents. It had been interested in the girl. Now it was interested in the person who'd come to read the girl's room. It wasn't finished here.
